My mom keeps saying that—well, she says a lot of things, most of them not helpful—but it’s been since then, since the day she left, that I’ve been struggling like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you're not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… guilty, maybe? Not like I did something wrong, exactly. More like I keep thinking I should be handling it better.\n\nThe “worth much” part—I don’t really know how to answer that. I’m still going to work, I still know I’m good at what I do, at least I used to be. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. My daughter used to say I could fix anything, and now the sink was dripping last week and I just stared at it for ten minutes like it was asking me a math question.\n\nSo… I guess I feel pretty useless sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not worth much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even if you haven't been doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I work sheet metal, so I’m used to being tired—kind of comes with the territory, you know? Some days you’re hauling stuff around and climbing ladders and you get home and your body’s just done.\n\nBut if you mean tired like… for no reason, I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to it that way. I guess I notice more what I’m not getting done, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I don’t know if that’s “lack of energy” or just me getting stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track, honestly. My jeans still fit, I think, but I don’t own a scale—well, I used to, but it got shoved in the closet when we redid the bathroom, and then my brother borrowed half my tools and somehow that scale ended up behind a box of Christmas stuff…\n\nSorry. Appetite-wise, I don’t know. Some days I eat whatever’s easiest and don’t think much about it. I haven’t noticed some big weight change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, nobody’s told me I’m talking slow or anything. Sometimes at work I feel like everyone’s moving too fast around me, but that might just be because job sites are always like that—some guy needs a ladder, somebody else is yelling about measurements, and then you can’t find the snips you just had in your hand two minutes ago.\n\nRestless… I don’t know. I can sit, I guess. I do get up and wander into the kitchen sometimes and forget why I went in there, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. I haven’t really noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or low most of the time, nearly every day, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since she left, it’s been bad. I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but most days I come home and the house just feels… dead. Like too quiet. I used to complain about her leaving cups everywhere and now I’d take ten cups on the counter, you know?\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before shifts, which is embarrassing because the guys are all walking in with their coolers and hard hats and I’m sitting there trying to get my face right. And then at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years—stuff I could do half asleep before. I snap at the younger guys too, and they don’t deserve it, mostly.\n\nI haven’t even been going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… I don’t see the point right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you were dead, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels really heavy and I just sit there staring at the wall, but “wished I was dead” sounds… I don’t know.\n\nDeath pops into your head when stuff falls apart, I guess. Like when I’m driving past the cemetery by the old grocery store—well, it’s not a grocery store anymore, they turned it into one of those dollar places—and I think about how quiet it must be, which is a weird thought. But I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and sorted it out in my head. It’s more like… I don’t want things to be like this. That’s the part I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself thinking or moving more slowly than usual lately, like your thoughts are sluggish or your body feels weighed down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… sometimes my head feels kind of full, like there’s too many tabs open, but I don’t know if that’s “slow.” At work if something takes me longer, it might just be because I’m stopping to double-check myself, you know? Sheet metal’s not really forgiving—measure wrong once and then you’re chasing that mistake all day.\n\nMy body… I don’t know. I’m tired a lot, but weighed down sounds dramatic. I still get up, still do what I have to do. It’s more like I sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I couldn’t tell you if I was moving slow or just… sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in most or all activities, including things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I don’t know about “completely,” because I still do things I have to do, but the stuff I used to actually want to do? No. I haven’t been going out on the water, and I haven’t touched my sculpture. It just feels like… what’s the point.\n\nSince my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house feels dead. Too quiet. And then I’ll just sit there instead of doing anything. I used to be the kind of person who needed a project going, like metal scraps laid out everywhere, and now even looking at it makes me tired. Same with the boat—usually that clears my head, but I don’t even bother.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your daughter left, have you noticed any days where you completely lost your appetite or found yourself eating much more than usual, even if you weren’t paying attention to it at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Food’s been kind of… in the background, I guess. Like, I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then I remember I was supposed to buy milk, or I notice the little magnet my daughter got me from that county fair is crooked, and then I’m thinking about that whole day instead of eating.\n\nBut completely lost my appetite? I’m not sure I’d say that. And eating a lot more than usual… I haven’t been keeping track. Some days I grab whatever’s easy, some days I realize it’s late and I didn’t really think about dinner. I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing or just me being scattered.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of energy or felt tired nearly every day since your daughter left, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired is kind of hard to measure, you know? Like some days at the shop I’ll be dragging, but then sheet metal work isn’t exactly sitting at a desk either. Even when we’re not doing anything huge, there’s always noise, lights, somebody asking where a tool went—half the time the grinder’s left plugged in and I’m the one noticing it.\n\nSince she left, I mean… I come home and I don’t exactly feel rested. But “nearly every day,” I’m not sure. I haven’t been keeping a tally. Sometimes I’m tired, sometimes I think I’m just… I don’t know, worn out from thinking too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're a failure or that you've let people down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody blames themselves for stuff sometimes, right? Like if something goes wrong at work, I’ll run it over in my head—did I measure wrong, did I miss something—but that’s just kind of how the shop is. You mess up a cut and suddenly the whole piece is trash, and then Kenny starts acting like he never made a mistake in his life, which is funny because he once—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a failure. I’ve had moments where I wonder if I did enough, or if I should’ve handled things differently, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing gives you pleasure anymore, not even things you used to really enjoy, most days since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s the part that scares me a little. I used to go out on the water, even just for an hour, and it would kind of reset my head. Or I’d mess around with that sculpture in the garage—scrap pieces, old cutoffs from jobs, stuff nobody else wanted. My daughter used to say it looked like junk until it suddenly didn’t, which… anyway.\n\nSince she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead. I’ll look at the boat or the tools and I just… don’t see the point. It’s not like I’m choosing not to enjoy it. It’s more like I can’t get myself there. Some days I just sit in the truck before work crying, and then I’m trying to pull myself together before anybody sees. So yeah, most days, the stuff I used to care about feels kind of empty now.\n\nDoctor: Has it felt like nothing at all gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, nearly every day, yeah. Since she left for basic, I come home and it’s like the whole house is just… dead quiet, and not in a peaceful way.\n\nI used to go out on the water, or work on that sculpture in the garage, and now I’ll just stand there looking at it like, what’s the point? And then I don’t do it. I just end up sitting there, or crying in my truck before a shift, trying to get myself straight before I walk in, because the younger guys don’t need to see me falling apart. Although they’re driving me nuts lately too—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nBut yes. The things I used to enjoy don’t really feel good right now. Not most days.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a day since your daughter left where nothing at all gave you pleasure—not even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there have been days like that. Since she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead, like the air got sucked out of it. I’ll look at the boat or that sculpture I was working on—well, it’s not really a sculpture yet, it’s more like a pile of metal nobody else understands—and I just… don’t see the point.\n\nAnd then it spills into everything. I’m crying in the truck before work, trying to get my face together before I go in, and I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I snap at the younger guys over stupid stuff, and then I feel bad about that too. But yeah, to answer you… yes. There’ve been days where nothing felt good.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like I’ll sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I don’t know if that’s “lack of energy” or just me getting stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track, honestly. My jeans still fit, I think, but I don’t own a scale—well, I used to, but it got shoved in the closet when we redid the bathroom, and then my brother borrowed half my tools and somehow that scale ended up behind a box of Christmas stuff…\n\nSorry. Appetite-wise, I don’t know. Some days I eat whatever’s easiest and don’t think much about it. I haven’t noticed some big weight change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, nobody’s told me I’m talking slow or anything. Sometimes at work I feel like everyone’s moving too fast around me, but that might just be because job sites are always like that—some guy needs a ladder, somebody else is yelling about measurements, and then you can’t find the snips you just had in your hand two minutes ago.\n\nRestless… I don’t know. I can sit, I guess. I do get up and wander into the kitchen sometimes and forget why I went in there, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. I haven’t really noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or low most of the time, nearly every day, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since she left, it’s been bad. I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but most days I come home and the house just feels… dead. Like too quiet. I used to complain about her leaving cups everywhere and now I’d take ten cups on the counter, you know?\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before shifts, which is embarrassing because the guys are all walking in with their coolers and hard hats and I’m sitting there trying to get my face right. And then at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years—stuff I could do half asleep before. I snap at the younger guys too, and they don’t deserve it, mostly.\n\nI haven’t even been going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… I don’t see the point right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you were dead, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels really heavy and I just sit there staring at the wall, but “wished I was dead” sounds… I don’t know.\n\nDeath pops into your head when stuff falls apart, I guess. Like when I’m driving past the cemetery by the old grocery store—well, it’s not a grocery store anymore, they turned it into one of those dollar places—and I think about how quiet it must be, which is a weird thought. But I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and sorted it out in my head. It’s more like… I don’t want things to be like this. That’s the part I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself thinking or moving more slowly than usual lately, like your thoughts are sluggish or your body feels weighed down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… sometimes my head feels kind of full, like there’s too many tabs open, but I don’t know if that’s “slow.” At work if something takes me longer, it might just be because I’m stopping to double-check myself, you know? Sheet metal’s not really forgiving—measure wrong once and then you’re chasing that mistake all day.\n\nMy body… I don’t know. I’m tired a lot, but weighed down sounds dramatic. I still get up, still do what I have to do. It’s more like I sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I couldn’t tell you if I was moving slow or just… sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in most or all activities, including things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I don’t know about “completely,” because I still do things I have to do, but the stuff I used to actually want to do? No. I haven’t been going out on the water, and I haven’t touched my sculpture. It just feels like… what’s the point.\n\nSince my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house feels dead. Too quiet. And then I’ll just sit there instead of doing anything. I used to be the kind of person who needed a project going, like metal scraps laid out everywhere, and now even looking at it makes me tired. Same with the boat—usually that clears my head, but I don’t even bother.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your daughter left, have you noticed any days where you completely lost your appetite or found yourself eating much more than usual, even if you weren’t paying attention to it at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Food’s been kind of… in the background, I guess. Like, I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then I remember I was supposed to buy milk, or I notice the little magnet my daughter got me from that county fair is crooked, and then I’m thinking about that whole day instead of eating.\n\nBut completely lost my appetite? I’m not sure I’d say that. And eating a lot more than usual… I haven’t been keeping track. Some days I grab whatever’s easy, some days I realize it’s late and I didn’t really think about dinner. I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing or just me being scattered.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of energy or felt tired nearly every day since your daughter left, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired is kind of hard to measure, you know? Like some days at the shop I’ll be dragging, but then sheet metal work isn’t exactly sitting at a desk either. Even when we’re not doing anything huge, there’s always noise, lights, somebody asking where a tool went—half the time the grinder’s left plugged in and I’m the one noticing it.\n\nSince she left, I mean… I come home and I don’t exactly feel rested. But “nearly every day,” I’m not sure. I haven’t been keeping a tally. Sometimes I’m tired, sometimes I think I’m just… I don’t know, worn out from thinking too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're a failure or that you've let people down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody blames themselves for stuff sometimes, right? Like if something goes wrong at work, I’ll run it over in my head—did I measure wrong, did I miss something—but that’s just kind of how the shop is. You mess up a cut and suddenly the whole piece is trash, and then Kenny starts acting like he never made a mistake in his life, which is funny because he once—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a failure. I’ve had moments where I wonder if I did enough, or if I should’ve handled things differently, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing gives you pleasure anymore, not even things you used to really enjoy, most days since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s the part that scares me a little. I used to go out on the water, even just for an hour, and it would kind of reset my head. Or I’d mess around with that sculpture in the garage—scrap pieces, old cutoffs from jobs, stuff nobody else wanted. My daughter used to say it looked like junk until it suddenly didn’t, which… anyway.\n\nSince she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead. I’ll look at the boat or the tools and I just… don’t see the point. It’s not like I’m choosing not to enjoy it. It’s more like I can’t get myself there. Some days I just sit in the truck before work crying, and then I’m trying to pull myself together before anybody sees. So yeah, most days, the stuff I used to care about feels kind of empty now.\n\nDoctor: Has it felt like nothing at all gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, nearly every day, yeah. Since she left for basic, I come home and it’s like the whole house is just… dead quiet, and not in a peaceful way.\n\nI used to go out on the water, or work on that sculpture in the garage, and now I’ll just stand there looking at it like, what’s the point? And then I don’t do it. I just end up sitting there, or crying in my truck before a shift, trying to get myself straight before I walk in, because the younger guys don’t need to see me falling apart. Although they’re driving me nuts lately too—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nBut yes. The things I used to enjoy don’t really feel good right now. Not most days.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a day since your daughter left where nothing at all gave you pleasure—not even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there have been days like that. Since she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead, like the air got sucked out of it. I’ll look at the boat or that sculpture I was working on—well, it’s not really a sculpture yet, it’s more like a pile of metal nobody else understands—and I just… don’t see the point.\n\nAnd then it spills into everything. I’m crying in the truck before work, trying to get my face together before I go in, and I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I snap at the younger guys over stupid stuff, and then I feel bad about that too. But yeah, to answer you… yes. There’ve been days where nothing felt good.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no pleasure in anything at all, not even things you used to really enjoy, and if so, how often has that happened since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s happened a lot since she left. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, but most days I come home and the house just feels dead, and I look at the boat or the sculpture stuff in the garage and I just think, why even bother?\n\nAnd then it kind of follows me around. I’ll be sitting in my truck before a shift crying, trying to pull it together so nobody sees, and then at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. Since she left for basic, there’ve been a lot of times where nothing really feels good, even the stuff that used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… over the past month, it’s been a lot. Not every single second, but most days I’d say I hit that place where nothing feels worth doing.

Since my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead. I used to go out on the water, or mess around with that sculpture in the garage—though honestly it mostly looks like scrap metal right now—and now I just stand there and think, what’s the point? Then I don’t do any of it.

I don’t know. I just feel like I’m coming apart over something everybody keeps telling me I should be proud of.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like going out on the water or working on your sculpture?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I mean, I used to love taking the boat out, even if it was just to sit there and not catch anything. And the sculpture, that was my thing, you know? I’ve had pieces of scrap set aside for it for months.\n\nBut since my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house feels dead, and then everything just kind of… drains out of me. I look at the boat or I look at the sculpture and I just think, what’s the point? Then I end up sitting there, or crying in my truck before work, and I’m missing steps on jobs I’ve done forever. It’s embarrassing. I snap at the younger guys too, which I hate, because they’re just there doing their jobs.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and has this affected your ability to function in areas of your life beyond work and hobbies, such as sleep, appetite, or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I guess. Since my daughter left for basic. That’s when it really started.\n\nI keep thinking I should be used to her being gone, because kids leave, right? My mom keeps saying that—well, she says a lot of things, most of them not helpful—but it’s been since then, since the day she left, that I’ve been struggling like this.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty or like you're not worth much lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… guilty, maybe? Not like I did something wrong, exactly. More like I keep thinking I should be handling it better.\n\nThe “worth much” part—I don’t really know how to answer that. I’m still going to work, I still know I’m good at what I do, at least I used to be. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. My daughter used to say I could fix anything, and now the sink was dripping last week and I just stared at it for ten minutes like it was asking me a math question.\n\nSo… I guess I feel pretty useless sometimes, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as not worth much.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel tired or have a lack of energy most days, even if you haven't been doing much?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I work sheet metal, so I’m used to being tired—kind of comes with the territory, you know? Some days you’re hauling stuff around and climbing ladders and you get home and your body’s just done.\n\nBut if you mean tired like… for no reason, I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to it that way. I guess I notice more what I’m not getting done, if that makes sense. Like I’ll sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I don’t know if that’s “lack of energy” or just me getting stuck in my own head.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight lately—like eating a lot less or more than usual, or losing or gaining weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track, honestly. My jeans still fit, I think, but I don’t own a scale—well, I used to, but it got shoved in the closet when we redid the bathroom, and then my brother borrowed half my tools and somehow that scale ended up behind a box of Christmas stuff…\n\nSorry. Appetite-wise, I don’t know. Some days I eat whatever’s easiest and don’t think much about it. I haven’t noticed some big weight change, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving or speaking more slowly than usual, or on the flip side, feeling restless or unable to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so? I mean, nobody’s told me I’m talking slow or anything. Sometimes at work I feel like everyone’s moving too fast around me, but that might just be because job sites are always like that—some guy needs a ladder, somebody else is yelling about measurements, and then you can’t find the snips you just had in your hand two minutes ago.\n\nRestless… I don’t know. I can sit, I guess. I do get up and wander into the kitchen sometimes and forget why I went in there, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing. I haven’t really noticed, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or low most of the time, nearly every day, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… since she left, it’s been bad. I don’t know if I’d say every minute, but most days I come home and the house just feels… dead. Like too quiet. I used to complain about her leaving cups everywhere and now I’d take ten cups on the counter, you know?\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before shifts, which is embarrassing because the guys are all walking in with their coolers and hard hats and I’m sitting there trying to get my face right. And then at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years—stuff I could do half asleep before. I snap at the younger guys too, and they don’t deserve it, mostly.\n\nI haven’t even been going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… I don’t see the point right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about death or dying, or wished you were dead, since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly. I mean, I’ve had moments where everything feels really heavy and I just sit there staring at the wall, but “wished I was dead” sounds… I don’t know.\n\nDeath pops into your head when stuff falls apart, I guess. Like when I’m driving past the cemetery by the old grocery store—well, it’s not a grocery store anymore, they turned it into one of those dollar places—and I think about how quiet it must be, which is a weird thought. But I don’t know if that’s what you’re asking.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and sorted it out in my head. It’s more like… I don’t want things to be like this. That’s the part I know.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself thinking or moving more slowly than usual lately, like your thoughts are sluggish or your body feels weighed down?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… sometimes my head feels kind of full, like there’s too many tabs open, but I don’t know if that’s “slow.” At work if something takes me longer, it might just be because I’m stopping to double-check myself, you know? Sheet metal’s not really forgiving—measure wrong once and then you’re chasing that mistake all day.\n\nMy body… I don’t know. I’m tired a lot, but weighed down sounds dramatic. I still get up, still do what I have to do. It’s more like I sit down and then suddenly an hour’s gone, but I couldn’t tell you if I was moving slow or just… sitting there.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely lost interest in most or all activities, including things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess so. I don’t know about “completely,” because I still do things I have to do, but the stuff I used to actually want to do? No. I haven’t been going out on the water, and I haven’t touched my sculpture. It just feels like… what’s the point.\n\nSince my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house feels dead. Too quiet. And then I’ll just sit there instead of doing anything. I used to be the kind of person who needed a project going, like metal scraps laid out everywhere, and now even looking at it makes me tired. Same with the boat—usually that clears my head, but I don’t even bother.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your daughter left, have you noticed any days where you completely lost your appetite or found yourself eating much more than usual, even if you weren’t paying attention to it at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. Food’s been kind of… in the background, I guess. Like, I’ll open the fridge and just stand there, and then I remember I was supposed to buy milk, or I notice the little magnet my daughter got me from that county fair is crooked, and then I’m thinking about that whole day instead of eating.\n\nBut completely lost my appetite? I’m not sure I’d say that. And eating a lot more than usual… I haven’t been keeping track. Some days I grab whatever’s easy, some days I realize it’s late and I didn’t really think about dinner. I don’t know if that counts as appetite changing or just me being scattered.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt a lack of energy or felt tired nearly every day since your daughter left, even when you haven’t been doing much physically?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… tired is kind of hard to measure, you know? Like some days at the shop I’ll be dragging, but then sheet metal work isn’t exactly sitting at a desk either. Even when we’re not doing anything huge, there’s always noise, lights, somebody asking where a tool went—half the time the grinder’s left plugged in and I’m the one noticing it.\n\nSince she left, I mean… I come home and I don’t exactly feel rested. But “nearly every day,” I’m not sure. I haven’t been keeping a tally. Sometimes I’m tired, sometimes I think I’m just… I don’t know, worn out from thinking too much, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you're a failure or that you've let people down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, everybody blames themselves for stuff sometimes, right? Like if something goes wrong at work, I’ll run it over in my head—did I measure wrong, did I miss something—but that’s just kind of how the shop is. You mess up a cut and suddenly the whole piece is trash, and then Kenny starts acting like he never made a mistake in his life, which is funny because he once—\n\nSorry. I don’t know if I’d say I feel like a failure. I’ve had moments where I wonder if I did enough, or if I should’ve handled things differently, but I don’t know that it’s more than usual. It’s hard to separate it out.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like nothing gives you pleasure anymore, not even things you used to really enjoy, most days since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s the part that scares me a little. I used to go out on the water, even just for an hour, and it would kind of reset my head. Or I’d mess around with that sculpture in the garage—scrap pieces, old cutoffs from jobs, stuff nobody else wanted. My daughter used to say it looked like junk until it suddenly didn’t, which… anyway.\n\nSince she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead. I’ll look at the boat or the tools and I just… don’t see the point. It’s not like I’m choosing not to enjoy it. It’s more like I can’t get myself there. Some days I just sit in the truck before work crying, and then I’m trying to pull myself together before anybody sees. So yeah, most days, the stuff I used to care about feels kind of empty now.\n\nDoctor: Has it felt like nothing at all gives you pleasure anymore, even things you used to really enjoy, nearly every day since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, nearly every day, yeah. Since she left for basic, I come home and it’s like the whole house is just… dead quiet, and not in a peaceful way.\n\nI used to go out on the water, or work on that sculpture in the garage, and now I’ll just stand there looking at it like, what’s the point? And then I don’t do it. I just end up sitting there, or crying in my truck before a shift, trying to get myself straight before I walk in, because the younger guys don’t need to see me falling apart. Although they’re driving me nuts lately too—sorry, that’s not what you asked.\n\nBut yes. The things I used to enjoy don’t really feel good right now. Not most days.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a day since your daughter left where nothing at all gave you pleasure—not even things you used to really enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there have been days like that. Since she left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead, like the air got sucked out of it. I’ll look at the boat or that sculpture I was working on—well, it’s not really a sculpture yet, it’s more like a pile of metal nobody else understands—and I just… don’t see the point.\n\nAnd then it spills into everything. I’m crying in the truck before work, trying to get my face together before I go in, and I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I snap at the younger guys over stupid stuff, and then I feel bad about that too. But yeah, to answer you… yes. There’ve been days where nothing felt good.\n\nDoctor: Has there been a time when you felt absolutely no pleasure in anything at all, not even things you used to really enjoy, and if so, how often has that happened since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s happened a lot since she left. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute of every day, but most days I come home and the house just feels dead, and I look at the boat or the sculpture stuff in the garage and I just think, why even bother?\n\nAnd then it kind of follows me around. I’ll be sitting in my truck before a shift crying, trying to pull it together so nobody sees, and then at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I snap at the younger guys too, which… they get on my nerves, but they don’t deserve all that.\n\nSo, yeah. Since she left for basic, there’ve been a lot of times where nothing really feels good, even the stuff that used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced days where you felt no interest or pleasure in anything at all, not even things you used to enjoy, and if so, how frequently has this happened over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… over the past month, it’s been a lot. Not every single second, but most days I’d say I hit that place where nothing feels worth doing.\n\nSince my daughter left for basic, I come home and the house just feels dead. I used to go out on the water, or mess around with that sculpture in the garage—though honestly it mostly looks like scrap metal right now—and now I just stand there and think, what’s the point? Then I don’t do any of it.\n\nAnd it’s not just at home. I’m crying in the truck before shifts, trying to get myself together, and then I’m at work missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done forever. I snap at the younger guys too, which… I know isn’t fair. But yeah, the no-interest, no-pleasure thing has been happening most days this past month."
